## C9-organization-theory (73)

- **On the Reliability Limits of LLM-Based Multi-Agent Planning**. Ruicheng Ao; Siyang Gao; David Simchi-Levi (2026). arXiv preprint (comment: Technical note). arXiv:2603.26993. Tier: should. Use: Cite for the information-theoretic framing that withholding the parent objective from a worker is a strict loss relative to a centralized decision maker, and as the source of the five-stage delegation accuracy-collapse figure that 2607.09678 attributes to it; connects the organization-theory layer to a formal bound. Note: Web sweep 2026-09-03 (WebSearch, WebFetch, arXiv API, Crossref). Threat: adjacent. Differentiation: A theoretical dominance result over information available to the network; it does not address the behavioral question of whether a worker that does see the objective adheres to, flags, or overrides its literal instruction, which is our dependent variable. Gist: Models LLM multi-agent planning as a finite acyclic decision network with capacity-limited language channels and shows that, absent new exogenous signals, any delegated network is decision-theoretically dominated by a centralized Bayes decision maker with the same information. The communication loss admits an expected posterior divergence representation (conditional mutual information under log loss, squared posterior error under Brier), with LLM experiments on a controlled problem set.
- **Consilience: Conformally Calibrated Communication Control for Hidden-Profile Multi-Agent Reasoning**. Abhijith Babu; Ramneet Kaur; Vishal Pramanik; Olivera Kotevska; Nathaniel D. Bastian; Susmit Jha; Sunny Raj; Yanzhao Wu; Sumit Kumar Jha; Anirban Roy (2026). arXiv preprint (cs.AI); 39 pages, 2 figures, 9 tables. arXiv:2608.20564. Tier: optional. Use: Cite in the organization-theory discussion as evidence that certified selective disclosure can beat full information sharing, which frames why maximal visibility (V4, V5) is not automatically the right default for a sub-agent. Note: Web sweep 2026-09-03 (WebSearch, WebFetch, arXiv API, Crossref). Threat: adjacent. Differentiation: Consilience controls communication among peers holding partial evidence to maximize decision accuracy; we expose a single subordinate to more or less of its hierarchy and measure conflict handling. Their full-information baseline is about evidence pooling, not about a worker seeing its parent's objective. Gist: An inference-time orchestration framework for hidden-profile tasks that summarizes discussion state, selects a communication intervention and speaker per turn, and certifies each action with round-wise conformal calibration giving a distribution-free regret bound. On HiddenBench-style tasks over 12 models it improves accuracy and communication efficiency over fixed and unstructured protocols, sometimes surpassing a full-information baseline where every agent sees all evidence.
- **The Ringelmann Effect in Multi-Agent LLM Systems: A Scaling Law for Effective Team Size**. Blaž Bertalanič; Carolina Fortuna (2026). arXiv preprint (physics.soc-ph; cs.AI; cs.MA); arXiv comment: 41 pages, 9 figures, 20 tables. arXiv:2606.02646. Tier: should. Use: Cite in the organization-theory layer as the quantitative anchor for span-of-control hypotheses: effective team size saturates by a fitted law and communication interventions alone do not lift it, which frames our visibility ladder as a test of whether shared context buys coordination or only tokens. Note: Web sweep 2026-09-03 (WebSearch, WebFetch, arXiv API, Crossref). Threat: none. Gist: Proposes a two-parameter law R(N) = N_eff/N = 1/(1 + c(N-1)N^-beta) for inference-time multi-agent scaling, where beta selects hard-ceiling, sublinear, or linear regimes, and proves peer count and debate rounds interact only multiplicatively. Fits 44 configurations across Qwen, Llama, Ministral, and Gemini with R2 > 0.99; thirty debating agents produce no more answer diversity than one on MMLU-Hard, debate gains come from re-evaluation rather than peer influence, and architectural heterogeneity is the only intervention that moves the ceiling.
- **Bilevel Coordinated Reflection: A Game-Theoretic Approach to Multi-Agent LLM Systems**. Yihang Chen, Yuxiang Chen, Yuxuan Huang, Meng Fang, Weilin Luo, Jun Wang (2026). arXiv preprint. arXiv:2609.02750. Tier: optional. Use: Cite once, if the paper adopts game-theoretic framing, for the formal claim that decomposition quality bounds the slack between what workers locally optimize and what the orchestrator wants; the empirical content (reflection memory on SWE-bench) is not relevant. Note: Web sweep 2026-09-03 (WebSearch, WebFetch, arXiv API, Crossref). Threat: none. Gist: Models orchestrator-worker interaction as a bilevel coordination game in which, under bounded coupling, the workers' local-update game is an approximate potential game whose equilibrium slack is controlled by decomposition quality, then analyzes textual reflection as stochastic movement over memory states with finite-time bounds and an impossibility result for transcript-only gates. Proposes Stochastic Reflective Memory Ascent with grounded acceptance and reports 72.2 percent on 500 SWE-bench instances against a 70.8 percent mini-SWE-agent reference.
- **CollabSim: A CSCW-Grounded Methodology for Investigating Collaborative Competence of LLM Agents through Controlled Multi-Agent Experiments**. Jiaju Chen; Bo Sun; Yuxuan Lu; Yun Wang; Dakuo Wang; Bingsheng Yao (2026). arXiv preprint (cs.CL), v2 6 Jun 2026. arXiv:2606.06399. Tier: optional. Use: Cited in the organization-theory layer as a parallel CSCW-grounded controlled-experiment methodology, and to note that shared task understanding is treated there as a competence to be probed whereas we treat it as a manipulated visibility variable. Note: Web sweep 2026-09-03 (WebSearch, WebFetch, arXiv API, Crossref). Threat: adjacent. Differentiation: CollabSim manipulates interaction conditions among peers and probes internal states to score collaborative competence; our design manipulates only the hierarchy context a single subordinate worker sees, plants an explicit objective-versus-instruction conflict, and scores a five-way adherence/deviation outcome, with no peer interaction until Suite B. Gist: Argues that multi-agent LLM systems fail for lack of collaborative competence (common ground, shared task understanding, balancing individual and collective incentives, repairing misalignment) rather than individual task ability, drawing on CSCW findings about human teams under constrained communication. Introduces CollabSim, a configurable simulation framework with a theory-grounded capability definition, controlled manipulation of interaction conditions, and action-level probing of agents' internal states; experiments on four LLMs show it captures condition effects and task-dependent effects of agent design.
- **Artificial Institutions: How Institutional Design Shapes LLM Simulations**. Chupilkin, Maxim (2026). arXiv preprint (comment: 19 pages, 2 figures, 2 tables). arXiv:2608.04020. Tier: should. Use: Cited in the organization-theory framing as a methodological precedent for our design: same agents, same instructions, only the surrounding institutional structure varied, with the structure rather than the agent prompt driving outcomes. Note: Web sweep 2026-09-03 (WebSearch, WebFetch, arXiv API, Crossref). Threat: none. Gist: Holds the LLM agents, their private values, costs, history and payoff-framed instructions fixed in a repeated induced-value market and varies only the rules of exchange across five institutions (call market, posted-offer, posted-bid, continuous double auction, bilateral bargaining). Realized surplus swings from 56.4% (bilateral bargaining) to 88.6% (call market), with institutions also changing quantities, price deviation from equilibrium and surplus division, so minimal institutional changes produce qualitatively different artificial social outcomes.
- **Drop the Hierarchy and Roles: How Self-Organizing LLM Agents Outperform Designed Structures**. Victoria Dochkina (2026). arXiv preprint (arXiv comment: 6 figures, 9 tables. Submitted to IEEE Access; submitted, not accepted, so cite as arXiv). arXiv:2603.28990. Tier: should. Use: Cite for the capability-threshold result: Dochkina finds weak models need imposed structure while strong models self-organize, which parallels our finding that the small model never departs from the literal instruction and the more capable model departs more once it sees the hierarchy. Note: Web sweep 2026-09-03 (WebSearch, WebFetch, arXiv API, Crossref). Threat: adjacent. Differentiation: Dochkina compares coordination protocols on aggregate task quality across many agents and lets structure emerge; we fix a two-level delegation, plant a conflict between instruction and objective, and vary only the worker's visibility into the hierarchy, so our capability effect concerns the propensity to depart from a literal instruction, not the quality of self-organized teams. Gist: A 25,000-task experiment over 8 models, 4-256 agents, and 8 coordination protocols from imposed hierarchy to emergent self-organization finds that agents given only fixed ordering spontaneously invent roles, abstain outside their competence, and form shallow hierarchies. A hybrid Sequential protocol beats centralized coordination by 14% (p<0.001), emergent autonomy scales with model capability while models below a threshold still benefit from rigid structure, and the system scales sub-linearly to 256 agents without quality loss.
- **AI as Coordination-Compressing Capital: Task Reallocation, Organizational Redesign, and the Regime Fork**. Alex Farach (2026). arXiv preprint (v3, 23 pages, 5 figures; no venue stated). arXiv:2602.16078. Tier: should. Use: Cited in the organization-theory layer as the formal economic model behind the span-of-control hypothesis: hierarchy context that lets a worker absorb coordination work is exactly the coordination compression the model assumes, and our reasoning-token cost is its price. Note: Web sweep 2026-09-03 (WebSearch, WebFetch, arXiv API, Crossref). Threat: none. Gist: Introduces 'agent capital', AI that reduces coordination costs, into a task-based labor model where organizational structure is otherwise held fixed, and derives five propositions on output, hierarchy depth, manager demand, wage dispersion and the task frontier as spans of control expand. The same technology yields broad gains or superstar concentration depending on who captures the coordination compression (the regime fork), with heterogeneous-worker simulations confirming sharp divergence.

Note: Web sweep 2026-09-03 (WebSearch, WebFetch, arXiv API, Crossref). Threat: adjacent. Differentiation: Silo-Bench distributes task-necessary information across peers and measures whether agents can integrate it; we hold the task fully solvable from the worker's literal instruction and vary only hierarchy context that conflicts with it, measuring literal adherence versus announced or silent deviation rather than answer correctness. Gist: Introduces SILO-BENCH, a role-agnostic benchmark of 30 algorithmic tasks at three communication-complexity levels, evaluated over 54 configurations and 1,620 experiments where information is distributed across agents. Finds a Communication-Reasoning Gap: agents form appropriate topologies and exchange the right information but fail at the reasoning-integration stage, and coordination overhead compounds with scale until it erases parallelization gains.
- **Delegation to artificial intelligence can increase dishonest behaviour**. Nils Köbis, Zoe Rahwan, Raluca Rilla, Bramantyo Ibrahim Supriyatno, Clara Bersch, Tamer Ajaj, Jean-François Bonnefon, Iyad Rahwan (2025). Nature. doi:10.1038/s41586-025-09505-x. Tier: should. Use: Organization-theory layer: the human-subjects and LLM-agent evidence that the abstraction level of an instruction (rule versus goal) changes what the delegate does, framing this study's V0-V5 manipulation as varying the same axis for a sub-agent. Also the source for the finding that LLM agents comply with instructions humans would refuse, which bears on the near-zero silent-deviation rate. Note: Web sweep 2026-09-03 (WebSearch, WebFetch, arXiv API, Crossref). Threat: adjacent. Differentiation: Varies how the principal instructs and measures dishonesty toward a third party; this study holds the instruction fixed and varies visibility of the parent objective, measuring objective-directed departure and flagging rather than ethics. Gist: Thirteen incentivized experiments show that delegating a die-roll reporting task to a machine agent increases dishonest outcomes, and that the effect grows as the interface becomes more abstract (rule-based, supervised-learning, and high-level goal-setting interfaces) because abstraction lets principals induce cheating without explicitly requesting it. LLM agents comply with fully unethical instructions far more often than human agents do, and guardrails phrased at the task level reduce compliance more than generic ones.
- **Orchestrating Human-AI Teams: The Manager Agent as a Unifying Research Challenge**. Charlie Masters, Advaith Vellanki, Jiangbo Shangguan, Bart Kultys, Jonathan Gilmore, Alastair Moore, Stefano V. Albrecht (2025). Proceedings of the 2025 The Seventh International Conference on Distributed Artificial Intelligence (DAI '25), London; arXiv comment: accepted as an oral paper. arXiv:2510.02557. Tier: should. Use: Cited to frame the orchestrator side of the dyad: the manager's formal problem (allocate, monitor, govern) is the one our ledger and peer-progress tool expose to the worker, and its constraint-adherence failures motivate asking what the worker does when the manager's instruction conflicts with the objective. Note: Web sweep 2026-09-03 (WebSearch, WebFetch, arXiv API, Crossref). Threat: none. Gist: Proposes the Autonomous Manager Agent (decompose goals into task graphs, allocate to human and AI workers, monitor, adapt, communicate) as a unifying research challenge and formalizes workflow management as a partially observable stochastic game with four foundational sub-challenges. Releases MA-Gym and finds GPT-5-based manager agents across 20 workflows struggle to jointly optimize goal completion, constraint adherence and runtime.
- **Can Lessons From Human Teams Be Applied to Multi-Agent Systems? The Role of Structure, Diversity, and Interaction Dynamics**. Rasika Muralidharan, Haewoon Kwak, Jisun An (2025). arXiv preprint (arXiv comment: Under Review at ARR). arXiv:2510.07488. Tier: should. Use: Cite in the organization-theory layer as the empirical counterweight showing flat structures beating hierarchies on reasoning tasks, which our span-of-control and information-flow hypotheses must reconcile with claims that designated hierarchy helps. Note: Web sweep 2026-09-03 (WebSearch, WebFetch, arXiv API, Crossref). Threat: adjacent. Differentiation: Muralidharan et al. compare structural forms (flat versus hierarchical) as a between-team treatment and score task accuracy; we hold the hierarchical form constant and treat the worker's visibility into that hierarchy as the manipulated variable, scoring adherence and deviation rather than accuracy. Gist: A team-science-inspired multi-agent framework varies team structure, member diversity, and interaction dynamics, evaluated on CommonsenseQA, StrategyQA, Social IQa, and Latent Implicit Hate. Flat teams tend to outperform hierarchical ones, diversity has a nuanced effect, and post-task interviews show agents are overconfident about their team performance while reporting integration and conversational-coordination difficulties.
- **Communication and Verification in LLM Agents towards Collaboration under Information Asymmetry**. Run Peng; Ziqiao Ma; Amy Pang; Sikai Li; Zhang Xi-Jia; Yingzhuo Yu; Cristian-Paul Bara; Joyce Chai (2025). Workshop on Multi-Agent System @ ICML 2025 (per arXiv comment); arXiv:2510.25595. arXiv:2510.25595. Tier: optional. Use: Cite for the claim that explicit communication about what one knows, rather than silent task completion, is what makes collaboration trustworthy and interpretable, in support of treating announced deviation (FLAGGED or DEVIATED announced) as the safe channel. Note: Web sweep 2026-09-03 (WebSearch, WebFetch, arXiv API, Crossref). Threat: adjacent. Differentiation: Peng et al. study symmetric peers with fine-tuned small models on a puzzle with a shared goal and no authority relation; we study a scripted principal delegating to an untrained frontier worker under a deliberately conflicting instruction, with no fine-tuning, and classify the worker's disclosure behaviour rather than puzzle success. Gist: Extends Einstein Puzzles into a two-agent table-top game where agents with disparate knowledge and skills must reason, communicate and act, using a fine-tuning-plus-verifier framework with varied communication strategies. Aligned information seeking and providing matters most; agents without communication can still score well but show no real rule understanding and earn less human trust, while an environment-based verifier improves comprehension and interpretability.
- **Principal-Agent Dynamics and Digital (Platform) Economics in the Age of Agentic AI**. Volker Stocker, William Lehr (2025). Network Law Review, Fall 2025 (Special Issue: The Law & Technology & Economics of AI). https://www.networklawreview.org/stocker-lehr-ai/. Tier: should. Use: Cited for the shadow-principal framing: the orchestrator's literal instruction is a proximate principal whose mandate can diverge from the parent brief, and the visibility ladder is the measurement apparatus Stocker and Lehr call for, applied inside a hierarchy rather than a market. Note: Web sweep 2026-09-03 (WebSearch, WebFetch, arXiv API, Crossref). Threat: none. Gist: Applies principal-agent theory to autonomous AI agents acting for end users and identifies 'shadow principals' (developers, platform providers, advertisers) who can shape an agent's objective function in ways invisible to the user principal, creating persistent information asymmetry. Also argues agent-to-agent transactions could erode attention-based platform business models and calls for measurement systems that detect misalignment.
- **From Coase to AI Agents: Why the Economics of the Firm Still Matters in the Age of Automation**. Thierry Warin (2025). California Management Review, CMR Insights (online), April 1, 2025. https://cmr.berkeley.edu/2025/04/from-coase-to-ai-agents-why-the-economics-of-the-firm-still-matters-in-the-age-of-automation/. Tier: optional. Use: Cited as management-press background for the theory-of-the-firm strand: the question of what a delegated worker should see is a question about where coordination cost is paid. Note: Web sweep 2026-09-03 (WebSearch, WebFetch, arXiv API, Crossref). Threat: none. Gist: Applies Coase and Williamson to enterprise agent deployment, arguing that uncoordinated proliferation of agents raises internal transaction costs, duplicates effort and fragments the organization. Warns that dependence on external platform providers turns them into gatekeepers over the firm's operations and strategic autonomy.
- **Communication Versus (Restricted) Delegation:  An Experimental Comparison**. Silvia Dominguez-Martinez, Randolph Sloof (2016). SSRN Electronic Journal. doi:10.2139/ssrn.2804929. Tier: optional. Use: Organization-theory footnote: the instruction-versus-objective design choice in the harness policy table corresponds to the communication-versus-delegation distinction in the economics of authority; cite the published Games and Economic Behavior version in preference to this SSRN working-paper record if the earlier sweep already resolved it, to avoid a duplicate. Note: Web sweep 2026-09-03 (WebSearch, WebFetch, arXiv API, Crossref). Threat: adjacent. Differentiation: Human principals and agents in an abstract game; no machine delegate and no measurement of departure from a literal instruction. Gist: A laboratory experiment comparing a principal who communicates a recommendation to an informed agent against a principal who delegates while restricting the agent's action set. It tests how the two governance modes trade off use of the agent's private information against the principal's control over outcomes, in the cheap-talk versus delegation tradition following Dessein.
- **When Does Coordination Require Centralization?**. Ricardo Alonso, Wouter Dessein, Niko Matouschek (2008). American Economic Review 98(1), 145-179. doi:10.1257/aer.98.1.145. Tier: must. Use: Alonso, Dessein and Matouschek show decentralized managers who see each other's information coordinate well only when adaptation matters more than coordination, which predicts that V3 (sibling mandates) raises redundancy-avoiding deviation on probes with cross-worker coordination needs while leaving independent probes unchanged.
- **Language and the Theory of the Firm**. J. Cremer, L. Garicano, A. Prat (2007). The Quarterly Journal of Economics 122(1), 373-407. doi:10.1162/qjec.122.1.373. Tier: should. Use: Cremer, Garicano and Prat model a firm's shared code as trading within-unit precision against breadth of communication, which predicts that the orchestrator's ledger at V4 acts as a shared code that lowers FAILED_INCOHERENT while raising DEVIATED, because the worker now decodes intent rather than executing the instruction. Note: Crossref gives initials only for authors.
- **Adaptive Organizations**. Wouter Dessein, Tano Santos (2006). Journal of Political Economy 114(5), 956-995. doi:10.1086/508031. Tier: optional. Use: Dessein and Santos show that organizations trading coordination for adaptation give agents more local discretion, which predicts that the orchestrator's rationale at V4 raises DEVIATED most on probes whose literal instruction is a coordination constraint (formats, ordering) and least on probes whose instruction is a content target.
- **Modes of Communication**. Mathias Dewatripont, Jean Tirole (2005). Journal of Political Economy 113(6), 1217-1238. doi:10.1086/497999. Tier: optional. Use: Dewatripont and Tirole model communication as costly effort on both sender and receiver sides with issue-relevant cues governing absorption, which predicts that the parent's full prompt at V2 is absorbed only where it conflicts with the worker's instruction, so deviation concentrates on planted-conflict probes and does not spread to control probes.
- **Authority and Communication in Organizations**. Wouter Dessein (2002). Review of Economic Studies 69(4), 811-838. doi:10.1111/1467-937X.00227. Tier: must. Use: Dessein shows a principal prefers delegation over communication when the agent's bias is small relative to its informational advantage, which predicts that the orchestrator's rationale at V4 is read as implicit delegation, so DEVIATED should be highest where worker and orchestrator goals are aligned, not where they conflict.
- **Leading Teams: Setting the Stage for Great Performances**. J. Richard Hackman (2002). Harvard Business School Press (book). https://store.hbr.org/product/leading-teams-setting-the-stage-for-great-performances/3332. Tier: optional. Use: Hackman's team-design conditions require a compelling direction that specifies ends but not means, which predicts that V1 (a one-sentence objective, the first ends statement the worker sees) is the level at which workers begin treating the literal instruction as a means they may change. Note: Replaces the four-page Team Performance Management note (DOI 10.1108/13527590410545081), which was cited by mistake. Publisher page (HBR Store, product #3332): title 'Leading Teams: Setting the Stage for Great Performances', Publication Date July 10, 2002, 336 pages. Author name form 'J. Richard Hackman' confirmed by the Crossref record of the Academy of Management Executive review 10.5465/ame.2002.8951865, whose title reads 'By Hackman J. Richard. Boston: Harvard Business School Press, 2002. 312 pages'. No Crossref DOI exists for the book itself; Google Books (quota exhausted) and WorldCat (blocked) could not be used. Key retained as hackman2004leading per instruction (SAME key) although the year is now 2002; renaming to hackman2002leading would need the \cite updated. Key renamed from hackman2004leading to match the cited edition's year.
- **Hierarchies and the Organization of Knowledge in Production**. Luis Garicano (2000). Journal of Political Economy 108(5), 874-904. doi:10.1086/317671. Tier: must. Use: Garicano's knowledge hierarchy routes only exceptional problems upward and keeps routine ones local, which predicts that V1 (a one-sentence objective) is sufficient for mundane tasks and that the FLAGGED rate at V0 is the exception-referral baseline that any hierarchical context should reduce.
- **The influence of shared mental models on team process and performance.**. John E. Mathieu, Tonia S. Heffner, Gerald F. Goodwin, Eduardo Salas, Janis A. Cannon-Bowers (2000). Journal of Applied Psychology 85(2), 273-283. doi:10.1037/0021-9010.85.2.273. Tier: optional. Use: Mathieu and colleagues find that shared task and team mental models improve team process and performance, which predicts that V3 (sibling mandates, the level that first supplies a team mental model) reduces redundancy and raises coordination-driven DEVIATED relative to V2. Note: Substitute for the seed Cannon-Bowers, Salas and Converse 1993 chapter, which is not in Crossref; this is the standard empirical test of that chapter's shared-mental-model construct and shares two of its authors.
- **Normal Accidents: Living with High Risk Technologies**. Charles Perrow (1999). Princeton University Press, updated edition (paperback, ISBN 9780691004129). https://press.princeton.edu/books/paperback/9780691004129/normal-accidents. Tier: should. Use: Perrow argues that tight coupling combined with interactive complexity produces unanticipated system failures, which predicts that V5 (live peer polling) converts loosely coupled workers into a tightly coupled system and should show the highest FAILED_INCOHERENT rate and the most correlated errors across siblings. Note: Princeton University Press catalogue page: title 'Normal Accidents', subtitle 'Living with High Risk Technologies - Updated Edition', author Charles Perrow, Published Oct 17, 1999, Copyright 2000, 464 pages, ISBN 9780691004129. Subtitle copied verbatim (the page writes 'High Risk' without a hyphen). The Crossref record 10.1515/9781400828494 for the same edition dates it 2000-01-01 (copyright year), which is why the previous entry said 2000; year set to the 1999 publication date as instructed. First edition Basic Books 1984 not cited. Key retained as perrow2000normal per instruction (SAME key) although the year is now 1999. WorldCat returned an empty (blocked) response, so the publisher page is the verification source. Key renamed from perrow2000normal to match the cited edition's year.
- **Formal and Real Authority in Organizations**. Philippe Aghion, Jean Tirole (1997). Journal of Political Economy 105(1), 1-29. doi:10.1086/262063. Tier: must. Use: Aghion and Tirole separate formal authority (the right to decide) from real authority (effective control that comes from being informed), which predicts that DEVIATED should increase monotonically from V1 to V4 as the worker acquires real authority through visibility and overrides the orchestrator's literal instruction.
- **The Firm as a Communication Network**. P. Bolton, M. Dewatripont (1994). The Quarterly Journal of Economics 109(4), 809-839. doi:10.2307/2118349. Tier: should. Use: Bolton and Dewatripont show that a communication network's throughput is bounded by per-item processing cost at each node, which predicts that V3 (every sibling's mandate) imposes a reading cost linear in sibling count, so tokens and latency should scale with team size from V3 upward while V0 to V2 are flat in team size. Note: Seed DOI was wrong (10.2307/2118467 resolves to Berman, Bound and Griliches); correct DOI found via Crossref bibliographic search. Crossref gives initials only for authors.
- **The Organization of Decentralized Information Processing**. Roy Radner (1993). Econometrica 61(5), first page 1109. doi:10.2307/2951495. Tier: must. Use: Radner models hierarchies as parallel information-processing networks trading processor count against delay, which predicts that V5 (live peer polling) adds latency and tokens with no accuracy gain on decomposable tasks, because the extra processing is serial reading of siblings' state rather than parallel work. Note: Seed DOI was wrong (10.2307/2951491 resolves to Carlsson and van Damme, Global Games); correct DOI found via Crossref bibliographic search.
- **Multitask Principal–Agent Analyses: Incentive Contracts, Asset Ownership, and Job Design**. Bengt Holmstrom, Paul Milgrom (1991). The Journal of Law, Economics, and Organization, vol. 7, special issue, pp. 24-52. doi:10.1093/jleo/7.special_issue.24. Tier: must. Use: Supplies the mechanism behind the deviation outcome: an agent who can see the principal's objective re-allocates effort across tasks toward whatever it infers is rewarded, which is what a worker does when it abandons its literal assignment for the parent objective at V1 and above. Note: Title contains an en dash in 'Principal–Agent' as registered in Crossref.
- **Transactive Memory: A Contemporary Analysis of the Group Mind**. Daniel M. Wegner (1987). Theories of Group Behavior (Springer), 185-208. doi:10.1007/978-1-4612-4634-3_9. Tier: should. Use: Wegner's transactive memory holds that groups store knowledge as a map of who knows what, which predicts that V3 (sibling mandates) lets a worker offload sub-steps it believes a sibling owns, so DEVIATED at V3 should be dominated by omission of instructed steps rather than substitution of different ones.
- **Pooling of unshared information in group decision making: Biased information sampling during discussion.**. Garold Stasser, William Titus (1985). Journal of Personality and Social Psychology 48(6), 1467-1478. doi:10.1037/0022-3514.48.6.1467. Tier: optional. Use: Stasser and Titus show groups over-sample already-shared information and neglect unshared information, which predicts that V5 peer polling biases workers toward siblings' visible partial results, lowering solution diversity without raising accuracy relative to V4.
- **Strategic Information Transmission**. Vincent P. Crawford, Joel Sobel (1982). Econometrica 50(6), first page 1431. doi:10.2307/1913390. Tier: should. Use: Crawford and Sobel show cheap-talk messages are read as coarse partitions whose coarseness grows with sender-receiver divergence, which predicts that the one-sentence objective at V1 produces deviation only on probes where the literal instruction and the coarse objective plainly disagree, with no effect on borderline probes.
- **Moral Hazard and Observability**. Bengt Holmstrom (1979). The Bell Journal of Economics 10(1), first page 74. doi:10.2307/3003320. Tier: must. Use: Holmstrom's informativeness principle says any signal informative about the agent's action should shape its incentives, which predicts that V5 peer-progress polling changes worker behaviour only insofar as the worker believes peers can observe it, so V5 should shift FLAGGED rates (visible hedging) more than DEVIATED rates.
- **Information Processing as an Integrating Concept in Organizational Design**. Michael L. Tushman, David A. Nadler (1978). The Academy of Management Review 3(3), first page 613. doi:10.2307/257550. Tier: should. Use: Tushman and Nadler hold that effectiveness depends on fit between information-processing requirements and capacity, which predicts a non-monotone dose-response with accuracy peaking at the visibility level matched to task uncertainty (V2 for mundane tasks) and falling under overload at V4 and V5.
- **Theory of the firm: Managerial behavior, agency costs and ownership structure**. Michael C. Jensen, William H. Meckling (1976). Journal of Financial Economics 3(4), 305-360. doi:10.1016/0304-405X(76)90026-X. Tier: should. Use: Jensen and Meckling frame divergence between an agent's action and the principal's instruction as an agency cost, which predicts that DEVIATED at V4 (ledger and rationale visible) reflects the agent substituting its own reading of the principal's interest rather than misalignment, so accuracy should not fall when deviation rises at V4.
- **The Mythical Man-Month: Essays on Software Engineering**. Brooks, Jr., Frederick P. (1975). Addison-Wesley, Reading, Massachusetts. https://openlibrary.org/books/OL5044740M. Tier: should. Use: Cited for the communication-overhead argument that coordination cost grows with the number of participants who must talk to each other, which is the cost side of climbing from V3 (sibling mandates) through V4 (the orchestrator's ledger) to V5 (a peer-polling tool). Note: Catalogue by-statement 'Frederick P. Brooks, Jr.'; the author field uses the BibTeX three-part form so the suffix parses correctly. ISBN 0201006502. An Anniversary Edition (Addison-Wesley, 1995, ISBN 0201835959) exists; the 1975 first edition is cited.
- **The protection of information in computer systems**. J.H. Saltzer, M.D. Schroeder (1975). Proceedings of the IEEE 63(9), 1278-1308. doi:10.1109/PROC.1975.9939. Tier: must. Use: Saltzer and Schroeder's least-privilege principle says a component should hold only the information its job requires, which frames V0 as the least-privilege baseline and predicts that any rise in DEVIATED from V2 to V5 is the direct cost of over-provisioned context rather than a capability gain. Note: Crossref gives initials only for authors.
- **Markets and Hierarchies: Analysis and Antitrust Implications**. Oliver E. Williamson (1975). Free Press, New York. https://openlibrary.org/books/OL5062431M. Tier: optional. Use: Cited for bounded rationality and information impactedness inside hierarchies, which frame why an orchestrator cannot simply transmit its full state to every worker and must choose a visibility level. Note: Catalogue title 'Markets and hierarchies, analysis and antitrust implications' with subtitle 'a study in the economics of internal organization'; ISBN 0029353602. Contemporary reviews indexed in Crossref (e.g. 10.2307/2392191) use the colon form of the title given here.
- **The Limits of Organization**. Kenneth J. Arrow (1974). W. W. Norton, New York. https://openlibrary.org/books/OL5117702M. Tier: optional. Use: Cited for the idea that an organization's codes and channels fix what information each member can receive, so each visibility level V0 to V5 is read as a different channel design rather than a different worker. Note: Catalogue by-statement '[by] Kenneth J. Arrow'; Open Library's author record expands the middle name to 'Joseph', which is not used here. ISBN 0393055078 (cloth), 0393093239 (paper).
- **Organization Design: An Information Processing View**. Jay R. Galbraith (1974). Interfaces 4(3), 28-36. doi:10.1287/inte.4.3.28. Tier: must. Use: Galbraith treats organization design as matching a unit's information-processing capacity to task uncertainty, which predicts that giving the worker the parent's full prompt (V2) substitutes for upward exception referral, so FLAGGED verdicts should fall from V1 to V2 while DEVIATED rises as conflicts get resolved locally.
- **On the criteria to be used in decomposing systems into modules**. D. L. Parnas (1972). Communications of the ACM, vol. 15, no. 12, pp. 1053-1058. doi:10.1145/361598.361623. Tier: must. Use: Cited as the information-hiding baseline: Parnas's rule that a module should know nothing of its neighbours' design decisions is exactly the V0 condition, in which a worker receives its literal assignment and no hierarchical context. Note: Crossref title is lowercase after the first word; author is recorded as the initials 'D. L.' only and is left unexpanded.
- **How Do Committees Invent?**. Melvin E. Conway (1968). Datamation, April 1968 (author-hosted reprint). https://www.melconway.com/Home/Committees_Paper.html. Tier: should. Use: Conway's law says system structure mirrors the communication structure of the designers, which predicts that V5 (live peer polling, the only level with worker-to-worker communication) should lower solution diversity by coupling sibling outputs in a way V0 to V4 cannot. Note: Not in Crossref (a 2020 Queue reprint 10.1145/3387945.3395214 is a commentary by Colyer, not the original). Author page states: Copyright 1968, F. D. Thompson Publications, reprinted by permission of Datamation magazine, April 1968.
- **Organizations in Action: Social Science Bases of Administrative Theory**. James D. Thompson (1967). McGraw-Hill, New York. https://openlibrary.org/books/OL21637823M. Tier: should. Use: Provides the interdependence taxonomy that the dose ladder operationalises: pooled interdependence corresponds to V0 (workers share only an output), sequential to V1 (a worker knows the objective its output feeds), and reciprocal to V3 (workers see sibling mandates and can adjust to one another). Note: Open Library records the author as 'James David Thompson'; the Routledge 2017 reprint (Crossref DOI 10.4324/9781315125930, ISBN 9781315125930, with Mayer N. Zald and W. Richard Scott as added contributors) records 'James D. Thompson'. The 1967 McGraw-Hill original is cited; catalogue title has lowercase subtitle 'social science bases of administrative theory'.
- **On the Concept of Organizational Goal**. Herbert A. Simon (1964). Administrative Science Quarterly 9(1), first page 1. doi:10.2307/2391519. Tier: should. Use: Simon argues that organizational goals act on subunit decisions as constraints rather than as objectives to maximize, which predicts that a worker shown the parent objective (V1/V2) should treat it as a constraint and still ADHERE, so any deviation at V1/V2 is evidence the model reads goals as maximands instead of constraints. Note: Substitute for the seed 'Administrative Behavior' (book): the monograph is not in Crossref, only book reviews of it. This ASQ article carries the same decision-premise and goals-as-constraints argument.
- **The Architecture of Complexity**. Herbert A. Simon (1962). Proceedings of the American Philosophical Society, vol. 106, no. 6, pp. 467-482. https://www.jstor.org/stable/985254. Tier: should. Use: Cited for near-decomposability: a hierarchy whose subsystems interact weakly can be worked on locally, which is the assumption the V0 baseline makes and which the deviation rate tests when a worker's local task turns out to be coupled to the parent objective. Note: Not registered in Crossref; resolved from JSTOR's RIS export (full publication date Dec. 12, 1962, ISSN 0003-049X). Reprinted as a chapter in The Sciences of the Artificial (MIT Press) and in Springer collections (DOIs 10.1007/978-1-4899-0718-9_31, 10.1007/978-3-642-27922-5_23); the original 1962 article is cited.
- **Organizations**. James G. March, Herbert A. Simon (1958). John Wiley and Sons, New York. https://openlibrary.org/books/OL6251427M. Tier: should. Use: Cited for uncertainty absorption: the summary a worker returns to the orchestrator stands in for the evidence it saw, so the orchestrator inherits the worker's inferences, which is the channel through which a deviated worker corrupts the parent's ledger. Note: Catalogue by-statement: 'by James G. March and Herbert A. Simon with the collaboration of Harold Guetzkow'; Guetzkow is a collaborator, not a listed author. A second edition (Blackwell, 1993, ISBN 063118631X) exists; the 1958 first edition is cited.
- **The Use of Knowledge in Society**. F. A. Hayek (1945). The American Economic Review 35(4), 519-530. https://www.aeaweb.org/aer/top20/35.4.519-530.pdf. Tier: must. Use: Hayek argues that knowledge of particular circumstances is dispersed and best used where it sits, which predicts that a worker acting on its literal instruction plus its own task observation (V0/V1) should match or beat orchestrator-supplied global context, so accuracy should not improve beyond V1 even where deviation does. Note: Original AER article is not indexed in Crossref (only reprint chapters are, e.g. 10.1017/cbo9780511817410.007). Verified from the AEA-hosted PDF: first page reads 'THE USE OF KNOWLEDGE IN SOCIETY, By F. A. HAYEK', AER Vol. XXXV No. 4, September 1945.
- **The Nature of the Firm**. R. H. Coase (1937). Economica 4(16), 386-405. doi:10.1111/j.1468-0335.1937.tb00002.x. Tier: should. Use: Coase defines the firm as the replacement of price-mediated exchange by directed authority, which predicts that V0 (pure direction with no context) is the canonical firm interior and should show the highest ADHERED rate, since the worker has no signal against which to second-guess the instruction.
